He is the Maintainer of justice. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him”*** — and the verse closes with the two names that seal every proof in this sūrah: ***“the Almighty, All-Wise”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the verse as the statement that God has made His oneness clear to His creation through proofs and signs, and that the angels have affirmed the same truth by declaring it, and that the people of knowledge among His servants have done likewise. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the testimony in the same order and adds what the witnesses testify to, that God alone is worthy of worship, and it describes the two kinds of evidence that support the claim, the signs in creation and the commands in revelation. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse as the greatest of all testimonies, and the reader is shown a court in which the judge is the first witness.

The Book points to the same meaning when it names God as sufficient as a witness of His own oneness, and the sūrah of the cow states the foundation of the whole religion in a verse that carries the same name at its centre: (2:255 — **“Neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him. To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth”**). The verse before the reader has gathered the angels and the scholars into the same testimony, and a reader who asks how a person can know that there is no god but God is being given three answers in one sentence, the signs in the world, the testimony of the unseen creation, and the agreement of the learned.

**THE TWO WITNESSES WHO STAND WITH HIM**

The verse does not leave the reader alone with an abstraction, since it names the company that affirms the same truth: ***“and so are the angels”*** ***“and people of knowledge. He is the Maintainer of justice. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the phrase about the angels as their declaration of God's oneness, and the phrase about the people of knowledge as the declaration of the scholars among human beings. Al-Mukhtaṣar adds that the scholars testify by their words and by their invitation to the same truth, so that knowledge in this verse is a public duty rather than a private possession. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse as a ranking of witnesses, and the reader is shown a chain that runs from the Creator through the unseen servants to the learned among human beings.

The Book shows the angels performing that duty in the sūrah of the fortieth sūrah, where the bearers of the throne praise their Lord and ask forgiveness for the believers: (40:7 — **“Those ˹angels˺ who carry the Throne and those around it glorify the praises of their Lord, have faith in Him, and seek forgiveness for the believers”**). The verse before the reader places the scholars in the same line of work, which is the strongest possible statement of what knowledge is for. A reader who has learned something about God has been conscripted into the same testimony, and the verse gives him no option to keep the knowledge to himself.

**THE ONE WHO MAINTAINS JUSTICE**

The middle of the verse gives the reason the testimony matters: ***“and people of knowledge. He is the Maintainer of justice. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the name as the statement that God stands firm in justice in everything He has created and commanded, and al-Mukhtaṣar reads it in the same way, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same description of a Lord whose rule is measured. The Book uses the word for the scales of justice in the sūrah of the cattle when it describes the standing of the community of the middle, and the reader who understands the name understands why the sūrah has spent so long on the arguments of the people who dispute.

The verse closes by repeating the statement of oneness and by naming two more attributes: ***“the Almighty, All-Wise”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the two names as the power that no force can resist and the wisdom that never errs, and the sūrah has used the same pair at the close of the verse about the shaping of a child in the womb. The repetition is deliberate, since the Book puts the same seal on its arguments wherever it makes them, and a reader who has followed the sūrah from its first verse has seen the pair twice in eighteen verses. Imagine a deed whose final line carries the same two signatures in every copy; the repetition is the point, because a document that is challenged in court is strongest when it has been attested by the same authority throughout.

---

## Verse 3:19

> Certainly, Allah’s only Way is Islam. Those who were given the Scripture did not dispute ˹among themselves˺ out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them. Whoever denies Allah’s signs, then surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**THE RELIGION THAT GOD ACCEPTS**

The verse names the road and then explains why people left it: ***“Certainly, Allah’s only Way is Islam. Those”*** ***“who were given the Scripture did not dispute ˹among themselves˺ out of mutual envy”*** ***“until knowledge came to them. Whoever denies Allah’s signs”*** ***“then surely Allah is swift in reckoning”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the opening sentence as the statement that the religion accepted by God is submission to Him alone, and he notes that the word describes the way of every prophet rather than the name of a new community. Ibn Kathīr reads the verse with the same breadth and gathers the earlier scriptures into the same meaning, so that the reader understands the sūrah's argument with the People of the Book to be a claim about continuity rather than a claim to novelty. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same sentence with the same sense of a road that was always there.

The Book shows the first of the submitters making the same confession at the beginning of the story, where Abraham is commanded to submit and answers with the words that every believer repeats: (2:131 — **“Submit ˹to My Will˺,” he responded, “I submit to the Lord of all worlds”**). The sūrah of ʿImrān will return to Abraham later and will say plainly that he was neither a Jew nor a Christian: (3:67 — **“Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; he submitted in all uprightness and was not a polytheist”**). The verse before the reader therefore says what the sūrah will prove, and the two verses together tell the reader that labels arrived after the submission did.

**WHY THE DISPUTE BEGAN**

The verse then describes the quarrel that divided the people who had already received the truth: ***“who were given the Scripture did not dispute ˹among themselves˺ out of mutual envy”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the words as the statement that the People of the Book did not differ among themselves until the knowledge had come to them, and that the motive was mutual envy rather than honest doubt. Ibn Kathīr reads the phrase in the same way and notes that the dispute was a dispute about status and precedence, since the arrival of the final prophet had touched the place each community held in the story. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson for the reader, that a disagreement begun after knowledge has arrived is a different thing from a disagreement caused by ignorance, and that the Book treats the first kind as a moral failure.

The Book describes the same pattern twice more in the sūrahs that follow, and it uses the same words each time: (2:213 — **“no one disputed the Scriptures except the very people who received them after clear proofs had come to them—out of jealousy”**). The sūrah of the kneeling also records the same sequence for the people of the earlier scripture: (45:17 — **“it was not until knowledge came to them that they differed out of mutual envy. Surely your Lord will judge between them on the Day of Judgment regarding their differences”**). The reiteration is a warning to the new community as much as a description of the old one, since the Book is telling the people of Madinah that the disease of envy infects a community after it has been given knowledge, not before.

**SWIFT IN RECKONING**

The verse closes with the sentence it has been building towards: ***“then surely Allah is swift in reckoning”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the ending as the statement that the people who deny the signs of God will find the reckoning quick, and he explains the swiftness as the fact that nothing delays God when the time arrives. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same phrase in the same way, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same warning at its close. The Book has used the same two words about the accounting at the end of a passage about spending in the path of God, where the reader is told that the reward is paid quickly: (2:202 — **“Surely Allah is swift in reckoning”**).

The Book uses the phrase about God and about the final accounting in the same breath, which is why the closing words of this verse are placed after the argument with the People of the Book rather than before it. A reader who has followed the opening of the sūrah has been shown a court, a witness, a road and a dispute, and the sentence at the end of the verse brings the whole courtroom to order. Imagine a shopkeeper who discovers a forged bill a year after the customer has left the country; the verse tells the reader that the accounting of God does not have that problem, and the comfort and the threat are the same sentence.

---

## Verse 3:20

> So if they argue with you ˹O Prophet˺, say, “I have submitted myself to Allah, and so have my followers.” And ask those who were given the Scripture and the illiterate ˹people˺, “Have you submitted yourselves ˹to Allah˺?” If they submit, they will be ˹rightly˺ guided. But if they turn away, then your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants

**THE ANSWER TO AN ARGUMENT**

The verse hands the Prophet the reply he is to give when the argument comes: ***“So if they argue with you ˹O Prophet˺, say, “I have submitted myself to Allah”*** ***“and so have my followers.””***. Al-Jalalayn reads the words as the declaration that the Prophet has submitted himself to God and that the believers who follow him have done the same, so that the answer to a quarrel is a statement of where the speaker stands. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the verse as the instruction to announce submission plainly and to leave the argument behind. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse in the same way, and the reader is shown a messenger who does not win debates by debating but by describing the road he is on. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the passage in the light of the delegation that came from Najrān, whose exchange with the Prophet is the occasion the sūrah is known for.

The Book gives the same answer in its command about argument in the sūrah of the bee, where the believer is told how to speak to people who differ with him: (16:125 — **“Invite ˹all˺ to the Way of your Lord with wisdom and kind advice, and only debate with them in the best manner”**). The verse before the reader shows the manner being used by the Prophet himself, and the reader who has followed the sūrah through its proofs and its warnings sees the calm of the closing instruction. A community that argues with its neighbours needs a method, and the verse gives it two, a truthful statement about itself and a clear question to the other side.

**THE QUESTION PUT TO BOTH GROUPS**

The verse then widens the audience: ***“And ask those who were given the Scripture”*** ***“and the illiterate ˹people˺, “Have you submitted yourselves ˹to Allah˺?””***. Al-Jalalayn reads the two groups as the people who had received a scripture and the people who had none, and he notes that the Book speaks to both of them with the same question. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the verse as an invitation to the two kinds of community in Madinah, the learned and the unlettered, and it reads the question as the single point on which the whole sūrah turns. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same reach, and the reader is shown a messenger who does not distinguish between a scholar and a shepherd when the question is whether a person will submit to God.

The Book describes the same two populations in the sūrah of the Friday prayer, where the messenger is said to have been raised among the unlettered while the earlier scriptures remained in the hands of their keepers: (62:2 — **“raised for the illiterate ˹people˺ a messenger from among themselves—reciting to them His revelations, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom”**). The verse before the reader puts the two groups in one sentence for the first time in the sūrah, and the order of the sentence, the scripture-keepers first and the unlettered second, tells the reader that no one is being given a pass. A reader of either kind has been asked the same question by the end of the verse.

**THE DUTY THAT STOPS AT DELIVERY**

The verse then divides the future and removes a burden from the messenger: ***“If they submit, they will be ˹rightly˺ guided.”*** ***“But if they turn away”*** ***“then your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺.”*** ***“And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the two halves as the statement that the one who submits finds the road and that the one who turns away leaves the messenger with nothing but the delivery of the message, and al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same division in the same words. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same limit on the Prophet's responsibility, and the Book states the limit in the sūrah of the table with the same economy of language: (5:99 — **“The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal”**).

The Book tells the Prophet the same thing about the people he was most anxious for, and the sūrah of the cow carries the sentence that answers every preacher's despair: (2:272 — **“You are not responsible for people’s guidance ˹O Prophet˺—it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills”**). The verse before the reader closes with the name that has closed two of the earlier verses in this stretch, and the name is the reason the division is bearable. Imagine a teacher who has prepared the lesson, written it on the board and explained it twice, and who watches some students copy it down while others look out of the window; the bell will ring, the papers will be collected, and the teacher's duty was the lesson.

---

## Verse 3:21

> Indeed, those who deny Allah’s signs, kill the prophets unjustly, and kill people who stand up for justice—give them good news of a painful punishment

**A CHARGE THAT NAMES THREE CRIMES**

The verse describes a group whose record is complete: ***“Indeed, those who deny Allah’s signs, kill the prophets unjustly”***, and it adds the two crimes that follow, that they ***“and kill people who stand up”*** ***“for justice give them good news of a painful punishment”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the first charge as the denial of God's signs, and he reports the tradition that the people addressed here had a long record of killing prophets, since he says they are reported to have killed forty-three prophets and to have been forbidden from doing so by a hundred others who stood for justice. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same three charges and explains the motive behind the second and third, which is hatred of the truth and of the people who call for it. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse as a description of people who either killed the prophets themselves or supported those who did.

Ibn Kathīr reads the verse as the chastisement of the people who had been given a scripture and had turned it into a cover for the killing of the people who stood for justice. The Book records the same conduct against the same nation in two other sūrahs, and it uses the same words each time: (4:155 — **“breaking their covenant, rejecting Allah’s signs, killing the prophets unjustly, and for saying, “Our hearts are unreceptive!”—it is Allah Who has sealed their hearts for their disbelief, so they do not believe except for a few”**). The sūrah of the table records the pattern as a settled habit: (5:70 — **“they denied some and killed others”**).

**WHAT THE KILLERS OF PROPHETS ARE TOLD**

The verse ends with a sentence that sounds like its opposite: ***“for justice give them good news of a painful punishment”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the words as an announcement of a painful punishment, and he notes that the Book uses the language of good news here by way of mockery, since the people addressed had made a career of expecting good news for themselves. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same ending in the same way, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse as the announcement of what these murders have earned. The Book does not soften the sentence, and a reader who has followed the sūrah from its opening verses has seen the same uncompromising tone in the promises of defeat and the warnings of fire.

The Book uses the same kind of reversal in the sūrah of the cow, where the people who hid the revelation are told what they are really eating: (2:174 — **“consume nothing but fire into their bellies. Allah will neither speak to them on the Day of Judgment, nor will He purify them. And they will suffer a painful punishment”**). The verse before the reader adds the word for pain rather than the word for fire, and the difference is worth noticing, because a reader can picture a fire without picturing pain. The Book is careful to describe the consequence in the terms that a stubborn heart understands, and the people it is addressing had hearts that answered arguments with stones. Imagine a court that has to read a sentence to a man who has spent his life mocking the court; the reading itself is part of the sentence.

**THE DEEDS THAT DO NOT SURVIVE**

The next verse explains what the punishment costs the people who earned it, and it is a ledger entry of their own: (3:22 — **“They are the ones whose deeds are wasted in this world and the Hereafter. And they will have no helpers”**). Al-Jalalayn reads that verse as the statement that the works of these people are wasted in both lives, and al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same loss in the same way. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same sense of a wasted account, and the reader is shown that the people described in the previous verse were not idle; they had deeds, and the deeds did not survive the audit of their intentions.

The Book describes the same loss with the most striking image in the sūrah of the disk, where the good deeds of a certain kind of person are scattered like dust: (25:23 — **“whatever ˹good˺ deeds they did, reducing them to scattered dust”**). The Book gives the same people a question to answer in the sūrah of the cave: (18:103 — **“Shall we inform you of who will lose the most deeds”**). The verse before the reader uses the word for help in its second half, and the choice of that word is the reason the verse is so heavy. A person can lose a competition and console himself with the support of his family, and the verse tells the reader that the people whose deeds are wasted will have nobody left to call.

---

## Verse 3:22

> They are the ones whose deeds are wasted in this world and the Hereafter. And they will have no helpers

**A LEDGER WITH NOTHING LEFT IN IT**

The verse states the cost of the crimes described in the verse before it: ***“They are the ones”*** ***“whose deeds are wasted in this world”*** ***“and the Hereafter. And they will have no helpers”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the sentence as the statement that the works of these people have failed in both lives, and he explains that such a person has nothing left to bring to the reckoning, since the deeds he was counting on have already been discounted. Al-Mukhtaṣar gives the reason those deeds are worth nothing, which is the absence of faith, and it closes with the same promise of an end at which nobody will step forward to speak for them. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same loss, and the reader is shown that the Book measures a deed by the hand that holds it as much as by what the hand does.

The Book describes the same empty account in the sūrah of Hūd, where a certain kind of effort is said to perish in the present life and to leave nothing behind: (11:16 — **“Their efforts in this life will be fruitless and their deeds will be useless”**). The sūrah of the cow states the same outcome for people who traded the next life for this one, and it uses the word for help in a sentence that has the same shape as the verse before the reader: (2:86 — **“So their punishment will not be reduced, nor will they be helped”**).

**WHY A GOOD DEED CAN EVAPORATE**

The verse says that the deeds are wasted rather than that the deeds were never done, and the difference is the reason the Book returns to this subject so often. Al-Jalalayn reads the wasted works as the charity and kind treatment a person may have performed, which are real acts with real effects in the world and no standing at all in the accounting. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the words in the same way and names the missing element, which is faith, since a deed done without it belongs to a different account. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same sense of work that does not survive its author.

The Book uses an image for the same truth that a reader does not forget, and it places it in the sūrah of the light: (24:39 — **“their deeds are like a mirage in a desert, which the thirsty perceive as water, but when they approach it, they find it to be nothing”**). A traveller who runs towards water that does not exist has not been lazy; he has been busy in the wrong direction, and the Book's picture of a wasted life is the picture of a man who has walked a long way towards nothing. The reader is being taught to ask about the destination of his work rather than only about its quantity, and the question is the one the verse puts in front of every community that inherits a scripture. Imagine a builder who spends a year raising a wall on land that belongs to someone else; the wall is well made and it will have to come down.

**THE DAY WHEN NOBODY STEPS FORWARD**

The verse closes with the loss of support: ***“and the Hereafter. And they will have no helpers”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the phrase as the statement that these people will have no protectors to shield them from the punishment, and al-Mukhtaṣar reads it in the same way, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same absence of advocates. The Book has used the same sentence about the people whose deeds are wasted, and the repetition is a warning rather than a rhetorical flourish, since a reader who has been counting on the help of a family or a community is being told that the counting has an end.

The Book states the position of every creature on that day in a verse addressed to the community, and the sentence is short enough to memorise: (2:107 — **“you have no guardian or helper besides Allah”**). The verse before the reader names no punishment in particular, and the absence of detail is its own message, since a person who has been left to himself in a courtroom does not need to be told what happens next. The reader who has understood the verse begins to build his case in the years when the court is still open.

---

## Verse 3:23

> Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scriptures? Yet when they are invited to the Book of Allah to settle their disputes, some of them turn away heedlessly

**A PORTION THAT WAS NOT ENOUGH**

The verse turns to a group that had been given part of a scripture and then refused to let it judge: ***“Have you not seen those”*** ***“who were given a portion of the Scriptures?”*** ***“Yet when they are invited to the Book of Allah to settle their disputes, some of them turn away heedlessly”***. Al-Jalalayn explains the portion of the Book as a share of the Torah, and he reads the invitation as the call to let the Book of God judge the dispute that had arisen, and the turning away as the refusal of a group inside that community. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the verse as a description of people who had enough knowledge of the Torah to recognise the Prophet in it, and who nevertheless turned back when the Book was placed between them and their argument. Ibn ʿAbbās supplies the occasion, a dispute among the Jewish tribes of Madinah and Khaybar in which the parties came hoping for a ruling they could live with and then walked away from the ruling they were given.

The Book describes the same people in the sūrah of the cow, where the arrival of a messenger confirming what they already held produced the opposite of gratitude: (2:101 — **“cast the Book of Allah behind their backs as if they did not know”**). A reader who has followed the sūrah of ʿImrān through its arguments can see the picture forming, since the people who are said to have denied the signs and killed the prophets are now shown turning their faces from a book they still claim to honour.

**WHEN THE BOOK IS PUT ON THE TABLE**

The verb in this verse describes a procedure rather than a quarrel: a book is brought, the parties are called, and a ruling is expected to follow. Al-Jalalayn reads the phrase about being called as the moment when the case is placed before the Book of God, and he notes that the phrase describes the state of the people while they were being called rather than after. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same scene, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse as the description of a community that already knew what its own scripture said about the matter in question.

The Book puts the same question to the same people in the sūrah of the table, and the question is sharper than any accusation: (5:43 — **“why do they come to you for judgment when they ˹already˺ have the Torah containing Allah’s judgment, then they turn away after all? They are not ˹true˺ believers”**). The verse before the reader shows a party walking away from a book it had asked to be consulted, and the Book's response is to let the walking away stand as the answer. Imagine a family that has agreed to settle an inheritance dispute by reading the will their own grandfather wrote and signed; the reading takes five minutes, and the silence in the room afterwards is the family's own verdict on itself.

**WHAT THEY TRUST INSTEAD OF THE BOOK**

The reason for the refusal is given in the verse that follows, and it is a reason made of words: (3:24 — **“The Fire will not touch us except for a few days.” They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying”**). Al-Jalalayn reads that claim that the fire will touch these people only for a limited number of days, and he explains that the claim rests on a tradition their own scholars had invented rather than on anything their scripture said. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the verse as a warning that a claim like this is a lie spoken against God, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same sense of a community lulled by a comfortable story. The Book answers the claim plainly in the sūrah of the cow: (2:80 — **“The Fire will not touch us except for a number of days.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Have you taken a pledge from Allah—for Allah never breaks His word—or are you ˹just˺ saying about Allah what you do not know”**).

The Book then states what actually determines a person's fate, in a sentence that dismantles the whole structure of inherited privilege: (2:81 — **“engrossed in sin will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever”**). The verse before the reader is the hinge between the two ways of thinking, since a community that trusts a comfortable claim does not need to open the book it has been called to, and a community that opens the book finds out what the claim was worth. A reader who has understood this verse has learned to be suspicious of every religious sentence that requires no change of life.

---

## Verse 3:24

> This is because they say, “The Fire will not touch us except for a few days.” They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying

**THE CLAIM OF A FEW DAYS**

The verse gives the reason the previous community turned away from its own book: ***“This is because they say, “The Fire will not touch us except”*** ***“for a few days.” They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying”***. Al-Jalalayn explains the claim as a tradition that the fire would touch them only for a limited number of days, and he reports that the number held by these people was forty, which they had taken from the period their forefathers spent worshipping the calf. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same claim and names the confidence it produced, since a community that expects a short stay in the fire makes its plans accordingly. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same picture of a people who had arranged a light sentence for themselves and had stopped opening the book that would have corrected them.

The Book answers the same kind of claim in the sūrah of the cow, where two communities each reserved paradise for itself, and the answer asks for evidence rather than for more argument: (2:111 — **“These are their desires. Reply, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Show ˹me˺ your proof if what you say is true”**). The verse before the reader is the Book's second refusal of the same habit, and the reader is being taught that a hope is not a proof. A community that has invented a timetable for its own punishment has invented a religion to match, and the Book names the invention plainly.

**THE DELUSION THAT KEPT THEM WARM**

The second half of the verse names what the claim did to the people who held it: ***“for a few days.” They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the words as the statement that their own fabrications deceived them, and he notes the Arabic word for the deception, which carries the sense of a person being seduced by something he wants to be true. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same sentence and explains the effect of the belief, which made them bold against God and careless about His commands. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same portrait of confidence built on nothing, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the passage is directed at a community that had a scripture in hand and had stopped reading it in the one place where it had been contradicted.

The Book describes the same deception in the sūrah of the creator, where humanity is warned against letting the present life do the deceiving: (35:5 — **“So do not let the life of this world deceive you, nor let the Chief Deceiver deceive you about Allah”**). The Book also records a conversation in the sūrah of iron that is held after the opportunity is gone, when the people who trusted a comfortable hope are told what the hope was made of: (57:14 — **“were deluded by false hopes”**). The verse before the reader is doing the work of the conversation in advance, which is the reason it is placed in the middle of an argument with the people of the Book. Imagine a passenger who has been assured by a stranger that the train never leaves before noon; the station clock is in front of him, the timetable is on the wall, and the only thing keeping him seated is the story he prefers.

**WHAT THE CLAIM COST THEM**

The verse is the hinge of the passage, since it explains why the invitation of the previous verse was refused. Al-Jalalayn reads the connection as the statement that the turning away happened because of the claim, so that the refusal of the book and the invention about the fire are one act rather than two. The Book makes the same connection between a comfortable belief and a closed book in the sūrah of the heights, where a generation is described as inheriting the scripture and treating forgiveness as a settled entitlement: (7:169 — **“We will be forgiven ˹after all˺.” And if similar gain came their way”**).

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the verse in the setting of the sūrah and observes that the text has turned from the Christians to the Jews and is now addressing a specific claim that had been used to avoid the ruling of the Torah. The Book's method here is the method it uses with every community that reaches for a comfortable sentence, which is to quote the sentence and then test it. The reader who notices the method will stop looking for a sentence that makes his life easier and start looking for the sentence that is true, and the next verse supplies the standard by which both are measured.

---

## Verse 3:25

> But how ˹horrible˺ will it be when We gather them together on the Day about which there is no doubt—when every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged

**THE DAY THAT ENDS THE ARGUMENT**

The verse answers the comfortable claim of the previous verse with a question about the future: ***“But how ˹horrible˺ will it be”*** ***“when We gather them together on the Day about”*** ***“which there is no doubt”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the opening as a statement of how terrible the situation of these people will be when God gathers them on the day that nobody doubts, and he notes that the phrase about the day is a reminder rather than a new piece of information. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the verse as the announcement that the gathering will happen and that the claim about a few days will be replaced by an accounting. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same certainty, and the reader is shown that the Book's answer to a false hope is not an argument but a date.

The Book has already given the community the same date in the sūrah of the cow, in the last revelation of that sūrah, where the accounting is described in one sentence: (2:281 — **“you will ˹all˺ be returned to Allah, then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done”**). The verse before the reader repeats the same three ideas, the gathering, the payment and the absence of injustice, and the repetition tells the reader that the sūrah of ʿImrān is standing on the foundation the previous sūrah had laid. The sūrah of the cow also warned the community that on that day nobody will be able to help anybody, and the warning is stated there in the plainest terms: (2:48 — **“No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given”**).

**PAID IN FULL, WITH NOTHING LOST**

The second half of the verse names the two facts that make the day bearable for a believer and unbearable for the people who invented the few days: ***“when every soul will be paid in full”*** ***“for what it has done”*** ***“and none will be wronged”***. Al-Jalalayn reads the sentence as the statement that every soul will be given the full price of what it did, and he explains the last phrase as the promise that nobody will be wronged by so much as a fraction. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the same sentence and adds the reason it is stated so carefully, that the Book wants the reader to understand the justice of the court before he appears in it. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the verse with the same confidence in the accuracy of the accounting, and the reader is shown a ledger that has no missing pages.

The Book describes the same scales in the sūrah of the prophets, where the weighing is said to be exact and small enough to find a mustard seed: (21:47 — **“no soul will be wronged in the least. And ˹even˺ if a deed is the weight of a mustard seed, We will bring it forth. And sufficient are We as a ˹vigilant˺ Reckoner”**). The Book also tells the reader that the record itself will be shown, and that the surprised reader will find nothing absent from it: (18:49 — **“does not leave any sin, small or large, unlisted?” They will find whatever they did present ˹before them˺”**). A reader who has understood the three verses has understood why the Book keeps returning to this subject, since a person who is certain about the ledger arranges his affairs differently. Imagine a shopkeeper who keeps two sets of accounts, one for the inspector and one for himself, and who is told that the inspector has been reading both books all year.

**WHY THE WARNING IS PLACED HERE**

The verse stands between the refusal of the previous passage and the passage that follows, and the place is chosen. The people who had assured themselves of a few days in the fire were being shown the day when the arrangement of the days will be settled, and the sūrah is about to move on to the delegation that argued with the Prophet about the family of ʿImrān. Al-Jalalayn notes that the Book often closes an argument about the people of the Book with a reminder of the accounting, and the reader can see the pattern forming in the sūrah.

The Book uses the same closing move in the sūrah of the cow, where a passage about the People of the Book ends with the warning that they traded God's covenant for a small price: (3:77 — **“will have no share in the Hereafter. Allah will neither speak to them, nor look at them, nor purify them on the Day of Judgment. And they will suffer a painful punishment”**). The verse before the reader names no group and gives no detail of a punishment, and its plainness is the reason it can be read by anyone. A person who has never made the claim of the few days still stands in front of the same gathering, and the verse is spoken to him in the same voice.

---
